title: "Adam Optimizer"
question: "You use <code>torch.optim.Adam(params, weight_decay=0.1)</code> and find that large-gradient weights are barely regularised. Why, and what should you use?"

html: "<section class=\"art\"><h2>The Adam optimiser: a step size for every weight</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Kingma and Ba, &quot;Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization&quot; (2014) · Loshchilov and Hutter, &quot;Decoupled Weight Decay Regularization&quot; (2017) · Goh, &quot;Why Momentum Really Works&quot;, Distill (2017) · Fanout 05.06</p><h3>Why this matters</h3>\n<p>Adam (in its AdamW form) trains nearly every Transformer, diffusion model and RL agent. It is plain gradient descent plus two moving averages from lesson 01.14. Once you see why each piece is there, the hyperparameters <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0528em;\">β</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">1</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" mtight\">2</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span></span></span></span> and weight decay stop being magic numbers.</p>\n<h3>The problem Adam solves</h3>\n<p>The last lesson showed that one learning rate must suit the steepest direction, so flat directions crawl. Real networks have millions of weights whose gradients differ in size by factors of thousands. A single learning rate cannot suit all of them.</p>\n<h3>Building Adam in three steps</h3>\n<p><strong>1. Momentum: smooth the direction.</strong> Keep a moving average of the gradient:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" Like a ball rolling downhill, it builds speed along a long valley and stops zig-zagging across it.</p>\n<p><strong>2. RMSProp: scale each weight separately.</strong> Keep a moving average of the <em>squared</em> gradient:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span Adam&#39;s steps are roughly <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" <strong>direction</strong> (its sign, early on).</p>\n<h3>Worked example 2: two weights, very different gradients</h3>\n<p>Weight A gets gradients around 100; weight B around 0.01.</p>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>SGD</strong> with <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" class=\"mord\">0.001</span></span></span></span>: A moves 0.1 per step (maybe too much), B moves 0.00001 (barely at all).</li>\n<li><strong>Adam</strong> with <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" move about 0.001 per step, as long as their gradients are consistent.</li>\n</ul>\n<p>This is why Adam needs much less tuning than SGD on models with very different kinds of parameters, like embeddings next to attention weights.</p>\n<h3>Worked example 3: AdamW and weight decay</h3>\n<p>Weight decay shrinks weights a little each step: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" data-p=\"{&quot;lr&quot;:0.1}\"><div class=\"vz\"></div><figcaption>Three optimisers start at the same point in a long, narrow valley; the minimum is the black dot. Press run. SGD (coral) crawls along the valley; momentum (blue) builds speed; Adam (teal) rescales each direction. Change the learning rate and run again.</figcaption></figure>\n<h3>In code</h3>\n<pre class=\"code\"><code class=\"language-python\">import torch\ndef adam_step(w, g, m, v, t, lr=1e-3, b1=0.9, b2=0.999, eps=1e-8):\n    m = b1*m + (1-b1)*g\n    v = b2*v + (1-b2)*g*g\n    mh, vh = m/(1-b1**t), v/(1-b2**t)\n    return w - lr*mh/(vh.sqrt()+eps), m, v\n\nopt = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=3e-4, betas=(0.9, 0.95), weight_decay=0.1)\n</code></pre>\n<h3>Memory cost</h3>\n<p>Adam stores <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">m</span></span></span></span> and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span></span></span></span> for every weight: two extra copies of the model. For a 7-billion-parameter model in float32 that is 56 GB just for the optimiser state, which is why tricks like 8-bit Adam and ZeRO sharding exist.</p>\n<h3>Common mistakes</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Using <code>Adam(weight_decay=...)</code> and expecting AdamW behaviour.</strong> They differ; use <code>AdamW</code>.</li>\n<li><strong>An SGD-sized learning rate with Adam.</strong> 0.1 is normal for SGD and usually destroys an Adam run. Start around <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span Started at zero, the raw EMA takes about 20 steps to catch up. The bias-corrected version is right from the first step.</figcaption></figure>\n<h3>AdaGrad and RMSProp</h3>\n<p><strong>AdaGrad</strong> divides each coordinate&#39;s step by the square root of the <strong>sum</strong> of its squared gradients. Rarely-updated coordinates (rare words&#39; embeddings) keep large steps; frequently-updated ones slow down. Goodfellow et al. §8.5.1 note its flaw for deep learning: the sum only grows, so the effective learning rate decays to zero too early.</p>\n<p><strong>RMSProp</strong> replaces the sum with an EMA, so old gradients are forgotten and the step size can recover. Each coordinate&#39;s step is roughly <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" The decay is multiplied by <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">η</span></span></span></span> in PyTorch&#39;s implementation, so if you raise the learning rate you also raise the effective decay. Under a cosine schedule the decay fades with the learning rate. Exercise 3 makes you show the difference between Adam with L2 and AdamW on a problem where it matters.</p>\n<blockquote class=\"callout\"><p><strong class=\"callout-label\">In practice</strong> Use <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0.999</span></span></span></span> (smaller models), weight decay around 0.1 for transformers, and do not decay biases or norm gains.</p>\n</blockquote>\n<h3>Choosing an optimiser</h3>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §8.5.4 conclude that there is no consensus winner and that familiarity with the hyperparameters matters most. That is still roughly true. AdamW is the default for transformers. SGD with momentum remains competitive for convolutional networks on vision and sometimes generalises better. Newer optimisers (Lion, Sophia, Muon) are worth knowing about; Muon in particular has been used for some large-scale LLM training, but treat claims of large speedups sceptically until they reproduce at your scale.</p>\n</section><section class=\"art extra\"><div class=\"xlab\">Extra · goes beyond the Fanout lesson</div><h2>Gradient descent and conditioning</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> MML §7.1 · Boyd and Vandenberghe §9.3 · Goodfellow et al. §4.2-4.3, §8.2.1 · Gabriel Goh, Why Momentum Really Works · d2l.ai §12.3</p><h3>Why optimisation in ML is different</h3>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §8.1 open their optimisation chapter by pointing out that training a network is not pure optimisation. You care about performance on unseen data, but you can only minimise loss on the training set (<strong>empirical risk minimisation</strong>). You often minimise a <strong>surrogate</strong> loss (cross-entropy) instead of the metric you want (accuracy). And you estimate the gradient from a minibatch, so every step uses a noisy gradient. Keep these in mind; much of what follows is about making progress anyway.</p>\n<h3>Gradient descent on a quadratic</h3>\n<p>The cleanest way to understand any optimiser is to run it on a quadratic, because near a minimum every smooth loss looks like one:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span Below <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">λ</span></span></span></span> it decays smoothly; between <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">λ</span></span></span></span> and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">λ</span></span></span></span> it oscillates but still converges; above <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">λ</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0.08</span></span></span></span> it oscillates and grows.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>With <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">κ</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">100</span></span></span></span>, each step only removes about 2% of the error, so you need roughly 50 steps per factor of <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span></span></span></span>. Goh&#39;s article animates this: the iterate zig-zags across the steep direction while crawling along the shallow one. <strong>Conditioning, not dimension, is what makes optimisation slow.</strong> Boyd and Vandenberghe §9.3 prove the same rate for any strongly convex function whose Hessian eigenvalues lie between <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">m</span></span></span></span> and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" network</h3>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §8.2.1 describe the symptom: the gradient norm stays large while the loss barely moves, because the term <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" The fixes you will meet throughout the course are all ways to improve conditioning:</p>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Input and activation normalisation</strong> (standardised inputs, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) make the Hessian closer to a multiple of the identity.</li>\n<li><strong>Adaptive optimisers</strong> (Adam) rescale each coordinate, which acts like a diagonal preconditioner.</li>\n<li><strong>Good initialisation</strong> keeps the network in a region where curvature is moderate.</li>\n</ul>\n</section><section class=\"art extra\"><div class=\"xlab\">Extra · goes beyond the Fanout class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span></span></span></span>, as a function of condition number. Momentum turns a dependence on <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">κ</span></span></span></span> into a dependence on <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.04em;vertical-align:-0.2397em;\"></span><span class=\"mord sqrt\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8003em;\"><span class=\"svg-align\" style=\"top:-3em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\" style=\"padding-left:0.833em;\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">κ</span></span></span><span style=\"top:-2.7603em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:3em;\"></span><span class=\"hide-tail\" style=\"min-width:0.853em;height:1.08em;\"><svg xmlns=\"http://www.w3.org/2000/svg\" width=\"400em\" height=\"1.08em\" viewBox=\"0 0 400000 1080\" preserveAspectRatio=\"xMinYMin slice\"><path style=\"height:0.2397em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>: at <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">κ</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">0</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">4</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> that is about 5,000 steps vs 50.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>For <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">κ</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">100</span></span></span></span> that is roughly 0.82 per step instead of 0.98. This square-root improvement is the main reason momentum is used everywhere. Exercise 1 asks you to measure both rates and compare them with these formulas.</p>\n<p><strong>Nesterov momentum</strong> (Goodfellow et al. §8.3.3) evaluates the gradient at the look-ahead point <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6667em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0528em;\">β</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span></span></span></span> instead of at <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span></span></span></span>. It corrects course earlier and gives a better theoretical rate on convex problems; in deep learning the practical difference is small.</p>\n<p><strong>Two conventions.</strong> PyTorch&#39;s SGD computes <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">←</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0528em;\">β</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">g</span></span></span></span> and steps <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7778em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">−</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">η</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span></span></span></span> (as above). Some texts, including Goodfellow et al., write <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">←</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0528em;\">β</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">η</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">g</span></span></span></span> and step <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6667em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">+</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">v</span></span></span></span>. They are equivalent for a fixed learning rate but differ when <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">η</span></span></span></span> changes during training. When you compare against <code>torch.optim</code>, match its form exactly, as Exercise 2 requires.</p>\n</section></details>"
lead: "Adam (in its AdamW form) trains nearly every Transformer, diffusion model and RL agent. It is plain gradient descent plus two moving averages from lesson 01.14.
